Well, my regular doctor sent me here. He says my weight has dropped too low and everyone is just making such a huge fuss about it lately. But honestly, I don't see what the big deal is. Being this light, it actually helps me keep up with everything at the shop. I don't get slowed down, which is important because I'm a carpenter and we are swamped right now. 

Actually, we're doing this massive custom cabinetry job for a house out in the suburbs—beautiful cherry wood, really heavy stuff, but it has this gorgeous grain. I had to drive three hours just to source the right hardware for it last Tuesday because the client was being incredibly picky about the hinges. Antique brass, they said. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, yes, I think I'm perfectly fine. My husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin lately, but I keep telling them I’m fine and that people are just confusing discipline with sickness. 

Speaking of the apprentices, though, they just have zero discipline these days. Take Tommy, for instance. Just last week he completely ruined a whole set of dovetail joints on that cherry wood job because he wouldn't take ten minutes to properly sharpen his chisels. I always tell him, a dull chisel is way more dangerous than a sharp one. My grandfather taught me that when I was twelve. He had this beautiful old Arkansas whetstone, passed down from his dad, and we’d spend hours on Sunday afternoons just oiling and sharpening all the hand tools until they could shave the hair off your arm. You just don't see that kind of care anymore, you know? Everyone just grabs a power router and blasts through the wood. It makes such a mess of the workshop, dust everywhere... 

Wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the weight thing. But yeah, like I said, I'm completely fine.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... if I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. I just feel like I'd lose the grip I’ve worked so incredibly hard to keep, you know? 

But speaking of the holidays, we used to have these huge family dinners on this massive red oak table my uncle built. That table is actually what got me into carpentry in the first place. It had these beautifully turned legs, just solid, heavy wood. You really don't see red oak used like that much anymore because it's an absolute nightmare to transport, and the freight costs just completely eat into your profit margins. We had a couple in the shop just last month asking for a ten-foot solid oak farmhouse table, and I had to sit them down and explain that the shipping alone for the lumber would cost more than their chairs. People just don't understand logistics these days. They look at a picture on the internet and think they can just get it delivered next-day like it's a pair of shoes. 

Wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Gaining weight. But yeah, like I said, I just can't risk eating those heavy foods and losing my grip on things.

I don't get slowed down, which is important because I'm a carpenter and we are swamped right now. \n\nActually, we're doing this massive custom cabinetry job for a house out in the suburbs—beautiful cherry wood, really heavy stuff, but it has this gorgeous grain. I had to drive three hours just to source the right hardware for it last Tuesday because the client was being incredibly picky about the hinges. Antique brass, they said. Do you know how hard it is to find solid antique brass these days? Everything is just plated zinc now, it's terrible quality. You really have to know where to look. But anyway, yeah, I'm just here because my doctor wouldn't drop it about the weight thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, I think I'm perfectly fine. My husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin lately, but I keep telling them I’m fine and that people are just confusing discipline with sickness. \n\nSpeaking of the apprentices, though, they just have zero discipline these days. Take Tommy, for instance. Just last week he completely ruined a whole set of dovetail joints on that cherry wood job because he wouldn't take ten minutes to properly sharpen his chisels. I always tell him, a dull chisel is way more dangerous than a sharp one. My grandfather taught me that when I was twelve. He had this beautiful old Arkansas whetstone, passed down from his dad, and we’d spend hours on Sunday afternoons just oiling and sharpening all the hand tools until they could shave the hair off your arm. You just don't see that kind of care anymore, you know? Everyone just grabs a power router and blasts through the wood. It makes such a mess of the workshop, dust everywhere... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the weight thing. But yeah, like I said, I'm completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... if I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. I just feel like I'd lose the grip I’ve worked so incredibly hard to keep, you know? \n\nBut speaking of the holidays, we used to have these huge family dinners on this massive red oak table my uncle built. That table is actually what got me into carpentry in the first place. It had these beautifully turned legs, just solid, heavy wood. You really don't see red oak used like that much anymore because it's an absolute nightmare to transport, and the freight costs just completely eat into your profit margins. We had a couple in the shop just last month asking for a ten-foot solid oak farmhouse table, and I had to sit them down and explain that the shipping alone for the lumber would cost more than their chairs. People just don't understand logistics these days. They look at a picture on the internet and think they can just get it delivered next-day like it's a pair of shoes. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Gaining weight. But yeah, like I said, I just can't risk eating those heavy foods and losing my grip on things.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unattractive? I don't... I don't know if that's the right word. I just know I wouldn't look like *me* anymore. I've been this way for so long, and it's just how I'm built now. 

It's kind of like... well, it's like when you try to restore a piece of furniture and someone has slapped a thick coat of cheap latex paint over a beautiful mahogany veneer. We get those in the shop all the time. People find these incredible mid-century dressers at estate sales, and some DIYer thought it would look "cute" painted seafoam green. It just completely hides the true lines of the piece, you know? It takes hours and hours of stripping and sanding just to get down to the bare wood so you can actually see the craftsmanship again. The chemicals we have to use for that stripping process are just awful, too. You have to wear these heavy-duty respirators, and even then, the fumes give you this splitting headache by the end of the day. Tommy, my apprentice, he refuses to wear the respirator properly. He just lets it hang around his neck. I keep telling him he's going to ruin his lungs before he's thirty, but kids today just think they're invincible. 

Anyway... what was the question? Oh, about looking unattractive. Yeah, I just... I don't really think about it like that. I just want to look like myself.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Change who I am? I... I suppose it would. I mean, my discipline, my routine, it's all tied up in how I maintain myself. If I lost that control, I just wouldn't be the same person. 

It's like when you lose the true square on a project. Once your foundation is off, everything else you build on top of it is just going to be crooked. I was actually just explaining this to my husband the other day when we were trying to fix the deck out back. The original contractors didn't set the footings below the frost line, so over the last ten winters, the whole structure has just heaved and warped. You can't just slap new decking boards on top of that and call it a day. You have to dig down, pour new concrete, and start from scratch. It's exhausting work, mixing concrete by hand in a wheelbarrow. We used to have this great little electric mixer, but the motor burnt out last spring when Tommy tried to mix a batch that was way too dry. I swear, that boy costs me more in broken equipment than he makes me in labor. 

I'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Something about changing who I am? But yes, I think it would change me. I just need things to stay exactly how they are.

I don't get slowed down, which is important because I'm a carpenter and we are swamped right now. \n\nActually, we're doing this massive custom cabinetry job for a house out in the suburbs—beautiful cherry wood, really heavy stuff, but it has this gorgeous grain. I had to drive three hours just to source the right hardware for it last Tuesday because the client was being incredibly picky about the hinges. Antique brass, they said. Do you know how hard it is to find solid antique brass these days? Everything is just plated zinc now, it's terrible quality. You really have to know where to look. But anyway, yeah, I'm just here because my doctor wouldn't drop it about the weight thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, I think I'm perfectly fine. My husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin lately, but I keep telling them I’m fine and that people are just confusing discipline with sickness. \n\nSpeaking of the apprentices, though, they just have zero discipline these days. Take Tommy, for instance. Just last week he completely ruined a whole set of dovetail joints on that cherry wood job because he wouldn't take ten minutes to properly sharpen his chisels. I always tell him, a dull chisel is way more dangerous than a sharp one. My grandfather taught me that when I was twelve. He had this beautiful old Arkansas whetstone, passed down from his dad, and we’d spend hours on Sunday afternoons just oiling and sharpening all the hand tools until they could shave the hair off your arm. You just don't see that kind of care anymore, you know? Everyone just grabs a power router and blasts through the wood. It makes such a mess of the workshop, dust everywhere... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the weight thing. But yeah, like I said, I'm completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... if I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. I just feel like I'd lose the grip I’ve worked so incredibly hard to keep, you know? \n\nBut speaking of the holidays, we used to have these huge family dinners on this massive red oak table my uncle built. That table is actually what got me into carpentry in the first place. It had these beautifully turned legs, just solid, heavy wood. You really don't see red oak used like that much anymore because it's an absolute nightmare to transport, and the freight costs just completely eat into your profit margins. We had a couple in the shop just last month asking for a ten-foot solid oak farmhouse table, and I had to sit them down and explain that the shipping alone for the lumber would cost more than their chairs. People just don't understand logistics these days. They look at a picture on the internet and think they can just get it delivered next-day like it's a pair of shoes. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Gaining weight. But yeah, like I said, I just can't risk eating those heavy foods and losing my grip on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Unattractive? I don't... I don't know if that's the right word. I just know I wouldn't look like *me* anymore. I've been this way for so long, and it's just how I'm built now. \n\nIt's kind of like... well, it's like when you try to restore a piece of furniture and someone has slapped a thick coat of cheap latex paint over a beautiful mahogany veneer. We get those in the shop all the time. People find these incredible mid-century dressers at estate sales, and some DIYer thought it would look \"cute\" painted seafoam green. It just completely hides the true lines of the piece, you know? It takes hours and hours of stripping and sanding just to get down to the bare wood so you can actually see the craftsmanship again. The chemicals we have to use for that stripping process are just awful, too. You have to wear these heavy-duty respirators, and even then, the fumes give you this splitting headache by the end of the day. Tommy, my apprentice, he refuses to wear the respirator properly. He just lets it hang around his neck. I keep telling him he's going to ruin his lungs before he's thirty, but kids today just think they're invincible. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about looking unattractive. Yeah, I just... I don't really think about it like that. I swear, that boy costs me more in broken equipment than he makes me in labor. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Something about changing who I am? But yes, I think it would change me. I just need things to stay exactly how they are.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your fear of gaining weight mostly about not being able to control your eating or your body?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fear_of_Weight_Gain\nmanifestation: \"If I eat anything heavy or let myself have the foods we used to make for holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight and lose the grip I’ve worked so hard to keep.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I think it's both, really. Like I said before... if I let myself have those heavy holiday foods, or anything rich like that, I just start panicking that I'm going to gain weight and completely lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. It's all about keeping that grip. 

It reminds me of these bar clamps we use in the shop. You know, the really heavy-duty steel ones? When you're gluing up a big tabletop, you need absolute, unyielding pressure. If the clamp slips even a fraction of an inch, the whole joint is compromised. We had this huge batch of clamps we bought at an auction a few years ago, from this old cabinet maker who was retiring. Beautiful old tools, made back when they actually used quality steel. But half of them had these stripped threads. I spent three whole weekends trying to rethread them because I just couldn't stand the idea of throwing away good American steel. My husband kept telling me to just buy new ones, but the new ones they sell at the big box stores are just cheap aluminum garbage. You tighten them down and they just bow in the middle. You can't trust them to hold anything together when it really matters. 

Wait, what were you asking me? Oh, about controlling my eating. Yes, exactly. If I let go, even a little, I just worry I'll lose the grip on everything.

We get those in the shop all the time. People find these incredible mid-century dressers at estate sales, and some DIYer thought it would look \"cute\" painted seafoam green. It just completely hides the true lines of the piece, you know? It takes hours and hours of stripping and sanding just to get down to the bare wood so you can actually see the craftsmanship again. The chemicals we have to use for that stripping process are just awful, too. You have to wear these heavy-duty respirators, and even then, the fumes give you this splitting headache by the end of the day. Tommy, my apprentice, he refuses to wear the respirator properly. He just lets it hang around his neck. I keep telling him he's going to ruin his lungs before he's thirty, but kids today just think they're invincible. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about looking unattractive. Yeah, I just... I don't really think about it like that. I just want to look like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that gaining weight would change who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: Change who I am? I... I suppose it would. I mean, my discipline, my routine, it's all tied up in how I maintain myself. If I lost that control, I just wouldn't be the same person. \n\nIt's like when you lose the true square on a project. Once your foundation is off, everything else you build on top of it is just going to be crooked. I was actually just explaining this to my husband the other day when we were trying to fix the deck out back. The original contractors didn't set the footings below the frost line, so over the last ten winters, the whole structure has just heaved and warped. You can't just slap new decking boards on top of that and call it a day. You have to dig down, pour new concrete, and start from scratch. It's exhausting work, mixing concrete by hand in a wheelbarrow. We used to have this great little electric mixer, but the motor burnt out last spring when Tommy tried to mix a batch that was way too dry. I swear, that boy costs me more in broken equipment than he makes me in labor. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Something about changing who I am? But yes, I think it would change me. I just need things to stay exactly how they are.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about not being able to control your eating or your body?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it's both, really. Like I said before... if I let myself have those heavy holiday foods, or anything rich like that, I just start panicking that I'm going to gain weight and completely lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. It's all about keeping that grip. \n\nIt reminds me of these bar clamps we use in the shop. You know, the really heavy-duty steel ones? When you're gluing up a big tabletop, you need absolute, unyielding pressure. If the clamp slips even a fraction of an inch, the whole joint is compromised. We had this huge batch of clamps we bought at an auction a few years ago, from this old cabinet maker who was retiring. Beautiful old tools, made back when they actually used quality steel. But half of them had these stripped threads. I spent three whole weekends trying to rethread them because I just couldn't stand the idea of throwing away good American steel. My husband kept telling me to just buy new ones, but the new ones they sell at the big box stores are just cheap aluminum garbage. You tighten them down and they just bow in the middle. You can't trust them to hold anything together when it really matters. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, about controlling my eating. Yes, exactly. If I let go, even a little, I just worry I'll lose the grip on everything.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your weight is the one thing in your life that you can completely control?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Control... yeah, I suppose it is. Everything else is just so unpredictable, you know? But my weight, what I eat, that's mine. I can measure it, I can manage it. 

It's a lot like working with a good piece of hard maple. Maple is stubborn, but if your tools are sharp and you know what you're doing, it'll do exactly what you want it to. You can measure it down to the thirty-second of an inch and it holds its shape. Unlike pine. I hate working with pine. It's so soft, it dents if you just look at it wrong, and it warps the second the humidity changes. We had this client last month who insisted on a custom dining table made out of knotty pine because she saw it on some home renovation show. I tried to talk her out of it, told her it wouldn't hold up to daily use, especially with her three golden retrievers running around the house. Those dogs were absolute menaces, by the way. When we went over to take measurements, one of them chewed right through the cord of my orbital sander. I had to splice the wire back together in my truck just so we could finish the job. And the client didn't even apologize! Just laughed and said he was "teething." He was a full-grown dog! 

Anyway... what was the question? Oh, about control. Yeah, I just don't really know if it's the *only* thing I can control, but it's definitely the most important one.

We used to have this great little electric mixer, but the motor burnt out last spring when Tommy tried to mix a batch that was way too dry. I swear, that boy costs me more in broken equipment than he makes me in labor. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Something about changing who I am? But yes, I think it would change me. I just need things to stay exactly how they are.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about not being able to control your eating or your body?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it's both, really. Like I said before... if I let myself have those heavy holiday foods, or anything rich like that, I just start panicking that I'm going to gain weight and completely lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. It's all about keeping that grip. \n\nIt reminds me of these bar clamps we use in the shop. You know, the really heavy-duty steel ones? When you're gluing up a big tabletop, you need absolute, unyielding pressure. If the clamp slips even a fraction of an inch, the whole joint is compromised. We had this huge batch of clamps we bought at an auction a few years ago, from this old cabinet maker who was retiring. Beautiful old tools, made back when they actually used quality steel. But half of them had these stripped threads. I spent three whole weekends trying to rethread them because I just couldn't stand the idea of throwing away good American steel. My husband kept telling me to just buy new ones, but the new ones they sell at the big box stores are just cheap aluminum garbage. You tighten them down and they just bow in the middle. You can't trust them to hold anything together when it really matters. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, about controlling my eating. Yes, exactly. If I let go, even a little, I just worry I'll lose the grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your weight is the one thing in your life that you can completely control?\n\nPatient: Control... yeah, I suppose it is. Everything else is just so unpredictable, you know? But my weight, what I eat, that's mine. I can measure it, I can manage it. \n\nIt's a lot like working with a good piece of hard maple. Maple is stubborn, but if your tools are sharp and you know what you're doing, it'll do exactly what you want it to. You can measure it down to the thirty-second of an inch and it holds its shape. Unlike pine. I hate working with pine. It's so soft, it dents if you just look at it wrong, and it warps the second the humidity changes. We had this client last month who insisted on a custom dining table made out of knotty pine because she saw it on some home renovation show. I tried to talk her out of it, told her it wouldn't hold up to daily use, especially with her three golden retrievers running around the house. Those dogs were absolute menaces, by the way. When we went over to take measurements, one of them chewed right through the cord of my orbital sander. I had to splice the wire back together in my truck just so we could finish the job. And the client didn't even apologize! Just laughed and said he was \"teething.\" He was a full-grown dog! \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about control. Yeah, I just don't really know if it's the *only* thing I can control, but it's definitely the most important one.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you fear gaining weight because you think it would make you feel fat?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Feel fat? I... well, yes. It's like I was saying before about the holiday foods. If I eat anything heavy, anything rich like we used to make, I start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And then I'll just feel... big. Soft. Like I've lost that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep.

It's a lot like when wood gets waterlogged. You know, like if you leave a piece of untreated timber out in the rain? It just swells up and gets spongy. You can't work with it anymore. It won't take a clean edge, the saw blade just tears at it instead of cutting. We had this whole pallet of oak delivered last spring, and the delivery driver—this new kid who couldn't have been more than nineteen—just left it sitting right at the end of the driveway instead of bringing it under the awning. Of course, we got a freak thunderstorm that afternoon. Three inches of rain in two hours. The whole bottom layer of that pallet was ruined. Just soaked through. I tried putting it in the kiln we built in the corner of the shop, but it was too far gone. It took me a week just to build that kiln, by the way. Sourced all the insulation panels from a demolition site downtown. They were tearing down this beautiful old theater to build another generic apartment complex. It breaks my heart to see that kind of craftsmanship just bulldozed. 

I'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Getting off track. But yes, I worry about gaining weight. I just can't stand the thought of feeling heavy or losing my grip on it.

I had to drive three hours just to source the right hardware for it last Tuesday because the client was being incredibly picky about the hinges. Antique brass, they said. Do you know how hard it is to find solid antique brass these days? Everything is just plated zinc now, it's terrible quality. You really have to know where to look. He had this beautiful old Arkansas whetstone, passed down from his dad, and we’d spend hours on Sunday afternoons just oiling and sharpening all the hand tools until they could shave the hair off your arm. I just feel like I'd lose the grip I’ve worked so incredibly hard to keep, you know? \n\nBut speaking of the holidays, we used to have these huge family dinners on this massive red oak table my uncle built. That table is actually what got me into carpentry in the first place. It had these beautifully turned legs, just solid, heavy wood. You really don't see red oak used like that much anymore because it's an absolute nightmare to transport, and the freight costs just completely eat into your profit margins. We had a couple in the shop just last month asking for a ten-foot solid oak farmhouse table, and I had to sit them down and explain that the shipping alone for the lumber would cost more than their chairs. People just don't understand logistics these days. They look at a picture on the internet and think they can just get it delivered next-day like it's a pair of shoes. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Gaining weight. But yeah, like I said, I just can't risk eating those heavy foods and losing my grip on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Unattractive? I don't... I don't know if that's the right word. I just know I wouldn't look like *me* anymore. We get those in the shop all the time. People find these incredible mid-century dressers at estate sales, and some DIYer thought it would look \"cute\" painted seafoam green. The original contractors didn't set the footings below the frost line, so over the last ten winters, the whole structure has just heaved and warped. You can't just slap new decking boards on top of that and call it a day. You have to dig down, pour new concrete, and start from scratch. It's exhausting work, mixing concrete by hand in a wheelbarrow. We used to have this great little electric mixer, but the motor burnt out last spring when Tommy tried to mix a batch that was way too dry. I swear, that boy costs me more in broken equipment than he makes me in labor. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Something about changing who I am? But yes, I think it would change me. I just need things to stay exactly how they are.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about not being able to control your eating or your body?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it's both, really. Like I said before... if I let myself have those heavy holiday foods, or anything rich like that, I just start panicking that I'm going to gain weight and completely lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. It's all about keeping that grip. \n\nIt reminds me of these bar clamps we use in the shop. You know, the really heavy-duty steel ones? When you're gluing up a big tabletop, you need absolute, unyielding pressure. If the clamp slips even a fraction of an inch, the whole joint is compromised. We had this huge batch of clamps we bought at an auction a few years ago, from this old cabinet maker who was retiring. Beautiful old tools, made back when they actually used quality steel. But half of them had these stripped threads. I spent three whole weekends trying to rethread them because I just couldn't stand the idea of throwing away good American steel. My husband kept telling me to just buy new ones, but the new ones they sell at the big box stores are just cheap aluminum garbage. You tighten them down and they just bow in the middle. You can't trust them to hold anything together when it really matters. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, about controlling my eating. Yes, exactly. If I let go, even a little, I just worry I'll lose the grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your weight is the one thing in your life that you can completely control?\n\nPatient: Control... yeah, I suppose it is. Everything else is just so unpredictable, you know? But my weight, what I eat, that's mine. I can measure it, I can manage it. \n\nIt's a lot like working with a good piece of hard maple. Maple is stubborn, but if your tools are sharp and you know what you're doing, it'll do exactly what you want it to. You can measure it down to the thirty-second of an inch and it holds its shape. Unlike pine. I hate working with pine. It's so soft, it dents if you just look at it wrong, and it warps the second the humidity changes. We had this client last month who insisted on a custom dining table made out of knotty pine because she saw it on some home renovation show. I tried to talk her out of it, told her it wouldn't hold up to daily use, especially with her three golden retrievers running around the house. Those dogs were absolute menaces, by the way. When we went over to take measurements, one of them chewed right through the cord of my orbital sander. I had to splice the wire back together in my truck just so we could finish the job. And the client didn't even apologize! Just laughed and said he was \"teething.\" He was a full-grown dog! \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about control. Yeah, I just don't really know if it's the *only* thing I can control, but it's definitely the most important one.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight because you think it would make you feel fat?\n\nPatient: Feel fat? I... well, yes. It's like I was saying before about the holiday foods. If I eat anything heavy, anything rich like we used to make, I start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And then I'll just feel... big. Soft. Like I've lost that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep.\n\nIt's a lot like when wood gets waterlogged. You know, like if you leave a piece of untreated timber out in the rain? It just swells up and gets spongy. You can't work with it anymore. It won't take a clean edge, the saw blade just tears at it instead of cutting. We had this whole pallet of oak delivered last spring, and the delivery driver—this new kid who couldn't have been more than nineteen—just left it sitting right at the end of the driveway instead of bringing it under the awning. Of course, we got a freak thunderstorm that afternoon. Three inches of rain in two hours. The whole bottom layer of that pallet was ruined. Just soaked through. I tried putting it in the kiln we built in the corner of the shop, but it was too far gone. It took me a week just to build that kiln, by the way. Sourced all the insulation panels from a demolition site downtown. They were tearing down this beautiful old theater to build another generic apartment complex. It breaks my heart to see that kind of craftsmanship just bulldozed. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Getting off track. But yes, I worry about gaining weight. Look fat now? No, no I don't think I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep telling me I look too thin, but I keep telling them I'm perfectly fine. People just confuse discipline with sickness these days. Nobody understands what it takes to actually maintain standards anymore. 

It's just like how people treat their tools now. You go onto any job site, and you see these young kids tossing their chisels into a bucket like they're scrap metal. A good chisel, a really good high-carbon steel chisel, needs to be respected. You have to keep it oiled, you have to hone the edge after every major job. I have a set of Marples chisels I bought back in 1988, the ones with the split-proof handles. They don't even make them in Sheffield anymore, they moved production overseas and the quality just plummeted. But my set? I can still shave the hair off my arm with them. I spend at least an hour every Sunday evening just at my sharpening station. I built it myself, actually, out of some reclaimed mahogany I salvaged from an old bank lobby they were gutting. Beautiful wood, tight grain. The bank manager just wanted it all thrown in the dumpster so they could put up drywall and glass. Can you believe that? Throwing away century-old mahogany! 

Wait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight. Yeah, they say I'm too thin, but they just don't get it. I'm fine. It's just discipline.

We had a couple in the shop just last month asking for a ten-foot solid oak farmhouse table, and I had to sit them down and explain that the shipping alone for the lumber would cost more than their chairs. My husband kept telling me to just buy new ones, but the new ones they sell at the big box stores are just cheap aluminum garbage. You tighten them down and they just bow in the middle. If I let go, even a little, I just worry I'll lose the grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your weight is the one thing in your life that you can completely control?\n\nPatient: Control... yeah, I suppose it is. Everything else is just so unpredictable, you know? But my weight, what I eat, that's mine. I can measure it, I can manage it. \n\nIt's a lot like working with a good piece of hard maple. Maple is stubborn, but if your tools are sharp and you know what you're doing, it'll do exactly what you want it to. You can measure it down to the thirty-second of an inch and it holds its shape. Unlike pine. I hate working with pine. It's so soft, it dents if you just look at it wrong, and it warps the second the humidity changes. We had this client last month who insisted on a custom dining table made out of knotty pine because she saw it on some home renovation show. I tried to talk her out of it, told her it wouldn't hold up to daily use, especially with her three golden retrievers running around the house. Those dogs were absolute menaces, by the way. When we went over to take measurements, one of them chewed right through the cord of my orbital sander. I had to splice the wire back together in my truck just so we could finish the job. And the client didn't even apologize! Just laughed and said he was \"teething.\" He was a full-grown dog! \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about control. Yeah, I just don't really know if it's the *only* thing I can control, but it's definitely the most important one.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight because you think it would make you feel fat?\n\nPatient: Feel fat? And then I'll just feel... big. Soft. Like I've lost that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep.\n\nIt's a lot like when wood gets waterlogged. We had this whole pallet of oak delivered last spring, and the delivery driver—this new kid who couldn't have been more than nineteen—just left it sitting right at the end of the driveway instead of bringing it under the awning. Of course, we got a freak thunderstorm that afternoon. Three inches of rain in two hours. The whole bottom layer of that pallet was ruined. Just soaked through. I tried putting it in the kiln we built in the corner of the shop, but it was too far gone. It took me a week just to build that kiln, by the way. Sourced all the insulation panels from a demolition site downtown. They were tearing down this beautiful old theater to build another generic apartment complex. It breaks my heart to see that kind of craftsmanship just bulldozed. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Getting off track. But yes, I worry about gaining weight. I just can't stand the thought of feeling heavy or losing my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat now?\n\nPatient: Look fat now? No, no I don't think I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep telling me I look too thin, but I keep telling them I'm perfectly fine. People just confuse discipline with sickness these days. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep telling me I look too thin, but I keep telling them I'm perfectly fine. People just confuse discipline with sickness these days. Nobody understands what it takes to actually maintain standards anymore. \n\nIt's just like how people treat their tools now. You go onto any job site, and you see these young kids tossing their chisels into a bucket like they're scrap metal. A good chisel, a really good high-carbon steel chisel, needs to be respected. You have to keep it oiled, you have to hone the edge after every major job. I have a set of Marples chisels I bought back in 1988, the ones with the split-proof handles. They don't even make them in Sheffield anymore, they moved production overseas and the quality just plummeted. But my set? I can still shave the hair off my arm with them. I spend at least an hour every Sunday evening just at my sharpening station. I built it myself, actually, out of some reclaimed mahogany I salvaged from an old bank lobby they were gutting. Beautiful wood, tight grain. The bank manager just wanted it all thrown in the dumpster so they could put up drywall and glass. Can you believe that? Throwing away century-old mahogany! \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight. Yeah, they say I'm too thin, but they just don't get it. I'm fine. It's just discipline.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your weight is the only part of your body that you're satisfied with?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / carpenter / associates\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The only part? I... well, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm actually pretty satisfied with my hands. They're strong. Look at these calluses. You don't get hands like these unless you put in the hours and actually work for it. 

Like last week, I was doing this restoration on a beautiful Victorian vanity. Solid walnut. The client kept calling it "mid-century vintage" and I had to bite my tongue because, honestly, people just throw these words around without knowing the history of furniture making. Anyway, the dovetail joints on the drawers were starting to separate, and I had to carefully knock them apart with a rubber mallet. You have to be so incredibly gentle, or you'll crack the pins, and then you've ruined a piece of history. And the brass pulls on it! They were completely black with tarnish. I took them down to this little hardware store on 4th Street to see if I could find matching screws because a few of the original ones were stripped. 

Have you ever been to that store? Miller's Hardware? Well, don't bother looking for it now, because they just sold the building to a developer. It's going to be a boutique pet bakery or something ridiculous like that. Fifty years of a family business, just gone, all so people can buy organic cupcakes for their poodles. I went in there to say goodbye to the owner, Frank, and he just looked so tired. It's just... it's a real shame what's happening to the neighborhood. 

But um... I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about my body? Oh, right, being satisfied with it. Yeah, I don't know about my weight being the *only* thing. I just don't really think about the rest of it that much.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor sent me here. He says my weight has dropped too low and everyone is just making such a huge fuss about it lately. But honestly, I don't see what the big deal is. Being this light, it actually helps me keep up with everything at the shop. I don't get slowed down, which is important because I'm a carpenter and we are swamped right now. \n\nActually, we're doing this massive custom cabinetry job for a house out in the suburbs—beautiful cherry wood, really heavy stuff, but it has this gorgeous grain. I had to drive three hours just to source the right hardware for it last Tuesday because the client was being incredibly picky about the hinges. Antique brass, they said. Do you know how hard it is to find solid antique brass these days? Everything is just plated zinc now, it's terrible quality. You really have to know where to look. But anyway, yeah, I'm just here because my doctor wouldn't drop it about the weight thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, I think I'm perfectly fine. My husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin lately, but I keep telling them I’m fine and that people are just confusing discipline with sickness. \n\nSpeaking of the apprentices, though, they just have zero discipline these days. Take Tommy, for instance. Just last week he completely ruined a whole set of dovetail joints on that cherry wood job because he wouldn't take ten minutes to properly sharpen his chisels. I always tell him, a dull chisel is way more dangerous than a sharp one. My grandfather taught me that when I was twelve. He had this beautiful old Arkansas whetstone, passed down from his dad, and we’d spend hours on Sunday afternoons just oiling and sharpening all the hand tools until they could shave the hair off your arm. You just don't see that kind of care anymore, you know? Everyone just grabs a power router and blasts through the wood. It makes such a mess of the workshop, dust everywhere... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the weight thing. But yeah, like I said, I'm completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... if I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. I just feel like I'd lose the grip I’ve worked so incredibly hard to keep, you know? \n\nBut speaking of the holidays, we used to have these huge family dinners on this massive red oak table my uncle built. That table is actually what got me into carpentry in the first place. It had these beautifully turned legs, just solid, heavy wood. You really don't see red oak used like that much anymore because it's an absolute nightmare to transport, and the freight costs just completely eat into your profit margins. We had a couple in the shop just last month asking for a ten-foot solid oak farmhouse table, and I had to sit them down and explain that the shipping alone for the lumber would cost more than their chairs. People just don't understand logistics these days. They look at a picture on the internet and think they can just get it delivered next-day like it's a pair of shoes. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Gaining weight. But yeah, like I said, I just can't risk eating those heavy foods and losing my grip on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Unattractive? I don't... I don't know if that's the right word. I just know I wouldn't look like *me* anymore. I've been this way for so long, and it's just how I'm built now. \n\nIt's kind of like... well, it's like when you try to restore a piece of furniture and someone has slapped a thick coat of cheap latex paint over a beautiful mahogany veneer. We get those in the shop all the time. People find these incredible mid-century dressers at estate sales, and some DIYer thought it would look \"cute\" painted seafoam green. It just completely hides the true lines of the piece, you know? It takes hours and hours of stripping and sanding just to get down to the bare wood so you can actually see the craftsmanship again. The chemicals we have to use for that stripping process are just awful, too. You have to wear these heavy-duty respirators, and even then, the fumes give you this splitting headache by the end of the day. Tommy, my apprentice, he refuses to wear the respirator properly. He just lets it hang around his neck. I keep telling him he's going to ruin his lungs before he's thirty, but kids today just think they're invincible. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about looking unattractive. Yeah, I just... I don't really think about it like that. I just want to look like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that gaining weight would change who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: Change who I am? I... I suppose it would. I mean, my discipline, my routine, it's all tied up in how I maintain myself. If I lost that control, I just wouldn't be the same person. \n\nIt's like when you lose the true square on a project. Once your foundation is off, everything else you build on top of it is just going to be crooked. I was actually just explaining this to my husband the other day when we were trying to fix the deck out back. The original contractors didn't set the footings below the frost line, so over the last ten winters, the whole structure has just heaved and warped. You can't just slap new decking boards on top of that and call it a day. You have to dig down, pour new concrete, and start from scratch. It's exhausting work, mixing concrete by hand in a wheelbarrow. We used to have this great little electric mixer, but the motor burnt out last spring when Tommy tried to mix a batch that was way too dry. I swear, that boy costs me more in broken equipment than he makes me in labor. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Something about changing who I am? But yes, I think it would change me. I just need things to stay exactly how they are.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about not being able to control your eating or your body?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it's both, really. Like I said before... if I let myself have those heavy holiday foods, or anything rich like that, I just start panicking that I'm going to gain weight and completely lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. It's all about keeping that grip. \n\nIt reminds me of these bar clamps we use in the shop. You know, the really heavy-duty steel ones? When you're gluing up a big tabletop, you need absolute, unyielding pressure. If the clamp slips even a fraction of an inch, the whole joint is compromised. We had this huge batch of clamps we bought at an auction a few years ago, from this old cabinet maker who was retiring. Beautiful old tools, made back when they actually used quality steel. But half of them had these stripped threads. I spent three whole weekends trying to rethread them because I just couldn't stand the idea of throwing away good American steel. My husband kept telling me to just buy new ones, but the new ones they sell at the big box stores are just cheap aluminum garbage. You tighten them down and they just bow in the middle. You can't trust them to hold anything together when it really matters. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, about controlling my eating. Yes, exactly. If I let go, even a little, I just worry I'll lose the grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your weight is the one thing in your life that you can completely control?\n\nPatient: Control... yeah, I suppose it is. Everything else is just so unpredictable, you know? But my weight, what I eat, that's mine. I can measure it, I can manage it. \n\nIt's a lot like working with a good piece of hard maple. Maple is stubborn, but if your tools are sharp and you know what you're doing, it'll do exactly what you want it to. You can measure it down to the thirty-second of an inch and it holds its shape. Unlike pine. I hate working with pine. It's so soft, it dents if you just look at it wrong, and it warps the second the humidity changes. We had this client last month who insisted on a custom dining table made out of knotty pine because she saw it on some home renovation show. I tried to talk her out of it, told her it wouldn't hold up to daily use, especially with her three golden retrievers running around the house. Those dogs were absolute menaces, by the way. When we went over to take measurements, one of them chewed right through the cord of my orbital sander. I had to splice the wire back together in my truck just so we could finish the job. And the client didn't even apologize! Just laughed and said he was \"teething.\" He was a full-grown dog! \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about control. Yeah, I just don't really know if it's the *only* thing I can control, but it's definitely the most important one.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight because you think it would make you feel fat?\n\nPatient: Feel fat? I... well, yes. It's like I was saying before about the holiday foods. If I eat anything heavy, anything rich like we used to make, I start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And then I'll just feel... big. Soft. Like I've lost that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep.\n\nIt's a lot like when wood gets waterlogged. You know, like if you leave a piece of untreated timber out in the rain? It just swells up and gets spongy. You can't work with it anymore. It won't take a clean edge, the saw blade just tears at it instead of cutting. We had this whole pallet of oak delivered last spring, and the delivery driver—this new kid who couldn't have been more than nineteen—just left it sitting right at the end of the driveway instead of bringing it under the awning. Of course, we got a freak thunderstorm that afternoon. Three inches of rain in two hours. The whole bottom layer of that pallet was ruined. Just soaked through. I tried putting it in the kiln we built in the corner of the shop, but it was too far gone. It took me a week just to build that kiln, by the way. Sourced all the insulation panels from a demolition site downtown. They were tearing down this beautiful old theater to build another generic apartment complex. It breaks my heart to see that kind of craftsmanship just bulldozed. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Getting off track. But yes, I worry about gaining weight. I just can't stand the thought of feeling heavy or losing my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat now?\n\nPatient: Look fat now? No, no I don't think I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep telling me I look too thin, but I keep telling them I'm perfectly fine. People just confuse discipline with sickness these days. Nobody understands what it takes to actually maintain standards anymore. \n\nIt's just like how people treat their tools now. You go onto any job site, and you see these young kids tossing their chisels into a bucket like they're scrap metal. A good chisel, a really good high-carbon steel chisel, needs to be respected. You have to keep it oiled, you have to hone the edge after every major job. I have a set of Marples chisels I bought back in 1988, the ones with the split-proof handles. They don't even make them in Sheffield anymore, they moved production overseas and the quality just plummeted. But my set? I can still shave the hair off my arm with them. I spend at least an hour every Sunday evening just at my sharpening station. I built it myself, actually, out of some reclaimed mahogany I salvaged from an old bank lobby they were gutting. Beautiful wood, tight grain. The bank manager just wanted it all thrown in the dumpster so they could put up drywall and glass. Can you believe that? Throwing away century-old mahogany! \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight. Yeah, they say I'm too thin, but they just don't get it. I'm fine. It's just discipline.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only part of your body that you're satisfied with?\n\nPatient: The only part? I... well, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm actually pretty satisfied with my hands. They're strong. Look at these calluses. You don't get hands like these unless you put in the hours and actually work for it. \n\nLike last week, I was doing this restoration on a beautiful Victorian vanity. Solid walnut. Well, yeah, exactly. It's like I mentioned earlier... if I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just start panicking right away that I'm going to gain weight. It absolutely feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. 

Those holiday foods, though... my mother used to make this incredibly dense plum pudding. She'd steam it in this huge copper basin. Actually, copper is such a nightmare to maintain. We had a client last month who wanted a custom kitchen island with a hammered copper countertop. I tried to talk him out of it. I said, look, the patina is going to change unevenly unless you are meticulous about wiping up acid spills, like lemon juice or tomato sauce. But he wouldn't listen. He's an investment banker, one of those guys who wears a fleece vest over a dress shirt and thinks he knows everything about everything. 

He had this massive house up on the ridge, the kind with the fake stone veneer on the front that looks like it's going to peel off in a strong wind. I swear, the structural integrity of these new builds is an absolute joke. They use these engineered I-joists now instead of solid lumber, and they wonder why the floors bounce when you walk across the room. Back in the day, you'd use true dimensional lumber, rough-sawn fir or pine, and those houses are still standing a hundred years later without a single sag in the roofline. Now it's all oriented strand board and cheap glue. It's just disrespectful to the trades.

Wait, I'm doing it again. What was the question? Control? Right. Yeah, losing my grip on my weight. That's exactly it. I just can't let that happen.
