We get those in the shop all the time. People find these incredible mid-century dressers at estate sales, and some DIYer thought it would look \"cute\" painted seafoam green. It just completely hides the true lines of the piece, you know? It takes hours and hours of stripping and sanding just to get down to the bare wood so you can actually see the craftsmanship again. The chemicals we have to use for that stripping process are just awful, too. You have to wear these heavy-duty respirators, and even then, the fumes give you this splitting headache by the end of the day. Tommy, my apprentice, he refuses to wear the respirator properly. He just lets it hang around his neck. I keep telling him he's going to ruin his lungs before he's thirty, but kids today just think they're invincible. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about looking unattractive. Yeah, I just... I don't really think about it like that. I just want to look like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that gaining weight would change who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: Change who I am? I... I suppose it would. I mean, my discipline, my routine, it's all tied up in how I maintain myself. If I lost that control, I just wouldn't be the same person. \n\nIt's like when you lose the true square on a project. Once your foundation is off, everything else you build on top of it is just going to be crooked. I was actually just explaining this to my husband the other day when we were trying to fix the deck out back. The original contractors didn't set the footings below the frost line, so over the last ten winters, the whole structure has just heaved and warped. You can't just slap new decking boards on top of that and call it a day. You have to dig down, pour new concrete, and start from scratch. It's exhausting work, mixing concrete by hand in a wheelbarrow. We used to have this great little electric mixer, but the motor burnt out last spring when Tommy tried to mix a batch that was way too dry. I swear, that boy costs me more in broken equipment than he makes me in labor. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Something about changing who I am? But yes, I think it would change me. I just need things to stay exactly how they are.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about not being able to control your eating or your body?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it's both, really. Like I said before... if I let myself have those heavy holiday foods, or anything rich like that, I just start panicking that I'm going to gain weight and completely lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. It's all about keeping that grip. \n\nIt reminds me of these bar clamps we use in the shop. You know, the really heavy-duty steel ones? When you're gluing up a big tabletop, you need absolute, unyielding pressure. If the clamp slips even a fraction of an inch, the whole joint is compromised. We had this huge batch of clamps we bought at an auction a few years ago, from this old cabinet maker who was retiring. Beautiful old tools, made back when they actually used quality steel. But half of them had these stripped threads. I spent three whole weekends trying to rethread them because I just couldn't stand the idea of throwing away good American steel. My husband kept telling me to just buy new ones, but the new ones they sell at the big box stores are just cheap aluminum garbage. You tighten them down and they just bow in the middle. You can't trust them to hold anything together when it really matters. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, about controlling my eating. Yes, exactly. If I let go, even a little, I just worry I'll lose the grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your weight is the one thing in your life that you can completely control?\n\nPatient: Control... yeah, I suppose it is. Everything else is just so unpredictable, you know? But my weight, what I eat, that's mine. I can measure it, I can manage it. \n\nIt's a lot like working with a good piece of hard maple. Maple is stubborn, but if your tools are sharp and you know what you're doing, it'll do exactly what you want it to. You can measure it down to the thirty-second of an inch and it holds its shape. Unlike pine. I hate working with pine. It's so soft, it dents if you just look at it wrong, and it warps the second the humidity changes. We had this client last month who insisted on a custom dining table made out of knotty pine because she saw it on some home renovation show. I tried to talk her out of it, told her it wouldn't hold up to daily use, especially with her three golden retrievers running around the house. Those dogs were absolute menaces, by the way. When we went over to take measurements, one of them chewed right through the cord of my orbital sander. I had to splice the wire back together in my truck just so we could finish the job. And the client didn't even apologize! Just laughed and said he was \"teething.\" He was a full-grown dog! \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about control. Yeah, I just don't really know if it's the *only* thing I can control, but it's definitely the most important one.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight because you think it would make you feel fat?\n\nPatient: Feel fat? I... well, yes. It's like I was saying before about the holiday foods. If I eat anything heavy, anything rich like we used to make, I start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And then I'll just feel... big. Soft. Like I've lost that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep.\n\nIt's a lot like when wood gets waterlogged. You know, like if you leave a piece of untreated timber out in the rain? It just swells up and gets spongy. You can't work with it anymore. It won't take a clean edge, the saw blade just tears at it instead of cutting. We had this whole pallet of oak delivered last spring, and the delivery driver—this new kid who couldn't have been more than nineteen—just left it sitting right at the end of the driveway instead of bringing it under the awning. Of course, we got a freak thunderstorm that afternoon. Three inches of rain in two hours. The whole bottom layer of that pallet was ruined. Just soaked through. I tried putting it in the kiln we built in the corner of the shop, but it was too far gone. It took me a week just to build that kiln, by the way. Sourced all the insulation panels from a demolition site downtown. They were tearing down this beautiful old theater to build another generic apartment complex. It breaks my heart to see that kind of craftsmanship just bulldozed. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Getting off track. But yes, I worry about gaining weight. I just can't stand the thought of feeling heavy or losing my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat now?\n\nPatient: Look fat now? No, no I don't think I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep telling me I look too thin, but I keep telling them I'm perfectly fine. People just confuse discipline with sickness these days. Nobody understands what it takes to actually maintain standards anymore. \n\nIt's just like how people treat their tools now. You go onto any job site, and you see these young kids tossing their chisels into a bucket like they're scrap metal. A good chisel, a really good high-carbon steel chisel, needs to be respected. You have to keep it oiled, you have to hone the edge after every major job. I have a set of Marples chisels I bought back in 1988, the ones with the split-proof handles. They don't even make them in Sheffield anymore, they moved production overseas and the quality just plummeted. But my set? I can still shave the hair off my arm with them. I spend at least an hour every Sunday evening just at my sharpening station. I built it myself, actually, out of some reclaimed mahogany I salvaged from an old bank lobby they were gutting. Beautiful wood, tight grain. The bank manager just wanted it all thrown in the dumpster so they could put up drywall and glass. Can you believe that? Throwing away century-old mahogany! \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight. Yeah, they say I'm too thin, but they just don't get it. I'm fine. It's just discipline.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only part of your body that you're satisfied with?\n\nPatient: The only part? I... well, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm actually pretty satisfied with my hands. They're strong. Look at these calluses. You don't get hands like these unless you put in the hours and actually work for it. \n\nLike last week, I was doing this restoration on a beautiful Victorian vanity. Solid walnut. The client kept calling it \"mid-century vintage\" and I had to bite my tongue because, honestly, people just throw these words around without knowing the history of furniture making. Anyway, the dovetail joints on the drawers were starting to separate, and I had to carefully knock them apart with a rubber mallet. You have to be so incredibly gentle, or you'll crack the pins, and then you've ruined a piece of history. And the brass pulls on it! They were completely black with tarnish. I took them down to this little hardware store on 4th Street to see if I could find matching screws because a few of the original ones were stripped. \n\nHave you ever been to that store? Miller's Hardware? Well, don't bother looking for it now, because they just sold the building to a developer. It's going to be a boutique pet bakery or something ridiculous like that. Fifty years of a family business, just gone, all so people can buy organic cupcakes for their poodles. I went in there to say goodbye to the owner, Frank, and he just looked so tired. It's just... it's a real shame what's happening to the neighborhood. \n\nBut um... I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about my body? Oh, right, being satisfied with it. Yeah, I don't know about my weight being the *only* thing. I just don't really think about the rest of it that much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think gaining weight would make you feel like you're losing control over your body?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, exactly. It's like I mentioned earlier... if I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just start panicking right away that I'm going to gain weight. It absolutely feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nThose holiday foods, though... my mother used to make this incredibly dense plum pudding. She'd steam it in this huge copper basin. Actually, copper is such a nightmare to maintain. We had a client last month who wanted a custom kitchen island with a hammered copper countertop. I tried to talk him out of it. I said, look, the patina is going to change unevenly unless you are meticulous about wiping up acid spills, like lemon juice or tomato sauce. But he wouldn't listen. He's an investment banker, one of those guys who wears a fleece vest over a dress shirt and thinks he knows everything about everything. \n\nHe had this massive house up on the ridge, the kind with the fake stone veneer on the front that looks like it's going to peel off in a strong wind. I swear, the structural integrity of these new builds is an absolute joke. They use these engineered I-joists now instead of solid lumber, and they wonder why the floors bounce when you walk across the room. Back in the day, you'd use true dimensional lumber, rough-sawn fir or pine, and those houses are still standing a hundred years later without a single sag in the roofline. Now it's all oriented strand board and cheap glue. It's just disrespectful to the trades.\n\nWait, I'm doing it again. What was the question? Control? Right. Yeah, losing my grip on my weight. That's exactly it. I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that you are happy with about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or something really similar? About being satisfied with my body? I think I just said I really like my hands because they're strong from all the work I do. \n\nSpeaking of strong hands, you know what really takes hand strength? Hand-planing a piece of rough-cut oak down to a smooth finish. Most guys these days just run it through a thickness planer, flip a switch, and let the machine do the work. It's so loud, you have to wear those heavy earmuffs, and it just throws sawdust everywhere. But using a hand plane... a really good Stanley No. 4, maybe one from the 1930s before they started cheaping out on the castings... that's an art. You can feel the grain of the wood through the handles. You push it forward, and you get this beautiful, continuous ribbon of wood shaving coming up through the mouth of the plane. It sounds like a zipper, just this satisfying *shhhhck* sound. \n\nI found a gorgeous old Stanley at an estate sale last month. The guy who passed away, his widow was just selling off his entire shop for pennies. It broke my heart. She had a whole box of vintage auger bits marked for five dollars. Five dollars! Those things are worth their weight in gold if you know how to file the spurs right. I bought the whole box, of course, and the plane. The iron on the plane was completely rusted, but I soaked it in Evapo-Rust overnight and then spent three hours on my water stones bringing the edge back. \n\nSorry, I'm rambling about tools again. But yeah, you asked about my appearance and what I'm happy with. Like I said before, I don't really think my weight is the only thing. My hands are good. I just don't really focus on the rest of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the main thing that determines how attractive you are to others?\n\nPatient: Attractive to others? I... I don't really know. I don't think about being attractive to other people very much. I mean, my weight is important to me, but I don't know if that's what other people care about. \n\nHonestly, most of the people I interact with are clients, and they only care if I can finish their custom cabinets on time. Which, by the way, is getting harder and harder because getting good materials right now is a nightmare. I ordered some Baltic birch plywood three weeks ago from my usual supplier. Baltic birch is the best for cabinet boxes, hands down. It has more plies than standard plywood, no voids in the inner layers, so when you cut it, the edge looks clean and it holds screws perfectly. But my supplier called me on Tuesday and said it's backordered until next month, and offered me this cheap MDF core stuff instead. \n\nI told him, absolutely not. MDF is basically just heavy cardboard glued together. If it gets even a drop of water on it, it swells up like a sponge and you can never fix it. And it's so heavy! Trying to lift a 4x8 sheet of 3/4-inch MDF by yourself is a great way to throw out your back. I knew a guy, Tommy, used to work framing houses. He tried to muscle a sheet of MDF up a flight of stairs by himself, slipped, and the whole thing came down on him. Tore his rotator cuff. He had to be out of work for six months, and his wife had to take an extra shift at the diner just to pay their mortgage. \n\nAnyway, I had to drive two hours out of town to a specialty lumber yard just to find decent plywood. Cost me a fortune in gas, too. \n\nBut um... attractiveness? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you what makes me attractive to others. I just don't pay attention to that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the main thing that determines your overall health?\n\nPatient: My overall health? I mean... I don't really know. I guess it's a part of it, but I don't really think of it as the main thing. I try to stay active, keep moving, you know? \n\nSpeaking of moving, my knees have been killing me lately. I think it's from kneeling on concrete floors all week. We're doing this big restoration job on an old Victorian over on Elm Street. The floors were completely shot—water damage, termite rot, you name it. We had to rip up all the original heart pine, which was a crying shame. Heart pine is practically extinct now. You can only get it reclaimed from old factories and barns. The grain is so tight, and it has this deep, rich amber color that you just can't fake with modern stains. \n\nAnyway, the homeowner wanted us to lay down this new pre-finished engineered hardwood. I tried to explain to her that putting engineered wood in a house from 1890 is practically a crime against architecture. It just looks too perfect, too uniform. A house that old needs character, it needs imperfections. But she was dead set on it because she saw it on some home renovation show. Those shows are the worst thing to ever happen to my profession. They make it look like you can gut a kitchen and rebuild it in three days for five thousand dollars. They never show the plumbing nightmares, or the knob-and-tube wiring you have to rip out to bring things up to code. \n\nI spent three days last week just trying to level the subfloor in that house. The joists had sagged almost two inches in the center of the room. You can't just slap new flooring over that, or it'll feel like you're walking on a trampoline. I had to sister every single joist, which meant crawling around in a crawlspace that was maybe two feet high, covered in century-old dust and spider webs. \n\nBut um, what were we talking about? Health? Yeah, I don't know if my weight is the main thing. I just try to keep working. As long as I can still swing a hammer, I figure I'm doing okay.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Return the JSON now."

Trying to lift a 4x8 sheet of 3/4-inch MDF by yourself is a great way to throw out your back. I knew a guy, Tommy, used to work framing houses. He tried to muscle a sheet of MDF up a flight of stairs by himself, slipped, and the whole thing came down on him. Tore his rotator cuff. He had to be out of work for six months, and his wife had to take an extra shift at the diner just to pay their mortgage. \n\nAnyway, I had to drive two hours out of town to a specialty lumber yard just to find decent plywood. Cost me a fortune in gas, too. \n\nBut um... attractiveness? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you what makes me attractive to others. I just don't pay attention to that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the main thing that determines your overall health?\n\nPatient: My overall health? I mean... I don't really know. I guess it's a part of it, but I don't really think of it as the main thing. I try to stay active, keep moving, you know? \n\nSpeaking of moving, my knees have been killing me lately. I think it's from kneeling on concrete floors all week. We're doing this big restoration job on an old Victorian over on Elm Street. The floors were completely shot—water damage, termite rot, you name it. We had to rip up all the original heart pine, which was a crying shame. Heart pine is practically extinct now. You can only get it reclaimed from old factories and barns. The grain is so tight, and it has this deep, rich amber color that you just can't fake with modern stains. \n\nAnyway, the homeowner wanted us to lay down this new pre-finished engineered hardwood. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The only thing? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know I brought up my hands earlier, but I guess I feel pretty good about my arms and shoulders, too. You kind of have to have solid upper body strength to do what I do. 

Like just yesterday, I was maneuvering these massive slabs of live-edge black walnut. Have you ever seen raw black walnut? It's absolutely gorgeous, but it is incredibly dense and heavy. This client wants a ten-foot custom dining table, single slab. It took three of us just to get it off the flatbed truck and onto the sawhorses in the shop. The grain on this particular piece is just unbelievable, though. It has this crotch figure right near one end where the tree originally split into two branches, and it creates this sort of shimmering flame pattern in the wood. 

Most people these days just want to slather thick polyurethane all over a table like that, which completely kills the depth of the grain. I had to sit the clients down and explain that we have to do a hand-rubbed oil finish. You do a coat of pure tung oil, let it cure for a few days, buff it out with ultra-fine steel wool, and then do it all over again. It takes weeks to do it right, but the wood just ends up glowing from the inside out. Polyurethane basically just wraps the wood in cheap plastic. I will never understand why people pay thousands of dollars for a premium, historic piece of hardwood and then want it to feel like a Formica countertop from a diner. It completely defeats the purpose of having natural wood in your house. My old mentor, Frank, he used to refuse to even keep polyurethane in his shop. If a client asked for it, he'd show them the door. 

But, oh, sorry. You were asking about feeling good about my body. Yeah, so... no, my weight isn't the only thing. I like that my arms are strong enough to handle those heavy slabs without dropping them. I guess I just appreciate being able to do my job.

It's absolutely gorgeous, but it is incredibly dense and heavy. This client wants a ten-foot custom dining table, single slab. It took three of us just to get it off the flatbed truck and onto the sawhorses in the shop. The grain on this particular piece is just unbelievable, though. It has this crotch figure right near one end where the tree originally split into two branches, and it creates this sort of shimmering flame pattern in the wood. \n\nMost people these days just want to slather thick polyurethane all over a table like that, which completely kills the depth of the grain. I had to sit the clients down and explain that we have to do a hand-rubbed oil finish. You do a coat of pure tung oil, let it cure for a few days, buff it out with ultra-fine steel wool, and then do it all over again. It takes weeks to do it right, but the wood just ends up glowing from the inside out. Polyurethane basically just wraps the wood in cheap plastic. I will never understand why people pay thousands of dollars for a premium, historic piece of hardwood and then want it to feel like a Formica countertop from a diner. Losing control, definitely. It's not really about how I look to anyone else. It's just... if I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight and just completely lose this grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. 

Speaking of the holidays, my sister always hosts Thanksgiving now because I built her this massive dining table about ten years ago. It seats fourteen people easily. Solid white oak. White oak is incredible for dining furniture because it's a closed-grain wood, which makes it practically waterproof. That's actually why they use it to make whiskey barrels. You can spill a whole glass of red wine or gravy on that table and it just wipes right off without staining the fibers. 

I didn't use a single metal fastener on that entire piece, either. All traditional wooden joinery. I spent weeks cutting these huge mortise and tenon joints by hand with a Japanese pull saw and a set of antique chisels I bought at an estate sale. You really have to make sure your chisels are razor-sharp for that kind of work, or you'll just tear out the end grain and ruin the fit. I spend a good hour every Sunday just standing at my sharpening station with my water stones, working my way up to an 8000 grit stone until the steel edge is basically a mirror. It's tedious, sure, but when you slide a perfectly cut tenon into a mortise and it fits so tight you almost don't even need glue... there's just no feeling like it in the world. It's real, honest craftsmanship. 

But um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, the weight thing. Yeah, it's just about keeping my grip on things, I guess. I don't want to lose that.

Those things are worth their weight in gold if you know how to file the spurs right. I bought the whole box, of course, and the plane. But yeah, you asked about my appearance and what I'm happy with. Like I said before, I don't really think my weight is the only thing. My hands are good. I just don't really focus on the rest of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the main thing that determines how attractive you are to others?\n\nPatient: Attractive to others? I... I don't really know. I don't think about being attractive to other people very much. I mean, my weight is important to me, but I don't know if that's what other people care about. \n\nHonestly, most of the people I interact with are clients, and they only care if I can finish their custom cabinets on time. Which, by the way, is getting harder and harder because getting good materials right now is a nightmare. I ordered some Baltic birch plywood three weeks ago from my usual supplier. Baltic birch is the best for cabinet boxes, hands down. It has more plies than standard plywood, no voids in the inner layers, so when you cut it, the edge looks clean and it holds screws perfectly. But my supplier called me on Tuesday and said it's backordered until next month, and offered me this cheap MDF core stuff instead. \n\nI told him, absolutely not. MDF is basically just heavy cardboard glued together. If it gets even a drop of water on it, it swells up like a sponge and you can never fix it. And it's so heavy! Trying to lift a 4x8 sheet of 3/4-inch MDF by yourself is a great way to throw out your back. I knew a guy, Tommy, used to work framing houses. He tried to muscle a sheet of MDF up a flight of stairs by himself, slipped, and the whole thing came down on him. Tore his rotator cuff. He had to be out of work for six months, and his wife had to take an extra shift at the diner just to pay their mortgage. \n\nAnyway, I had to drive two hours out of town to a specialty lumber yard just to find decent plywood. Cost me a fortune in gas, too. \n\nBut um... attractiveness? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you what makes me attractive to others. I just don't pay attention to that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the main thing that determines your overall health?\n\nPatient: My overall health? I mean... I don't really know. I guess it's a part of it, but I don't really think of it as the main thing. I try to stay active, keep moving, you know? \n\nSpeaking of moving, my knees have been killing me lately. I think it's from kneeling on concrete floors all week. We're doing this big restoration job on an old Victorian over on Elm Street. The floors were completely shot—water damage, termite rot, you name it. We had to rip up all the original heart pine, which was a crying shame. Heart pine is practically extinct now. You can only get it reclaimed from old factories and barns. The grain is so tight, and it has this deep, rich amber color that you just can't fake with modern stains. \n\nAnyway, the homeowner wanted us to lay down this new pre-finished engineered hardwood. I tried to explain to her that putting engineered wood in a house from 1890 is practically a crime against architecture. It just looks too perfect, too uniform. A house that old needs character, it needs imperfections. But she was dead set on it because she saw it on some home renovation show. Those shows are the worst thing to ever happen to my profession. They make it look like you can gut a kitchen and rebuild it in three days for five thousand dollars. They never show the plumbing nightmares, or the knob-and-tube wiring you have to rip out to bring things up to code. \n\nI spent three days last week just trying to level the subfloor in that house. The joists had sagged almost two inches in the center of the room. You can't just slap new flooring over that, or it'll feel like you're walking on a trampoline. I had to sister every single joist, which meant crawling around in a crawlspace that was maybe two feet high, covered in century-old dust and spider webs. \n\nBut um, what were we talking about? Health? Yeah, I don't know if my weight is the main thing. I just try to keep working. As long as I can still swing a hammer, I figure I'm doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that makes you feel good about your body?\n\nPatient: The only thing? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know I brought up my hands earlier, but I guess I feel pretty good about my arms and shoulders, too. You kind of have to have solid upper body strength to do what I do. \n\nLike just yesterday, I was maneuvering these massive slabs of live-edge black walnut. Have you ever seen raw black walnut? It's absolutely gorgeous, but it is incredibly dense and heavy. This client wants a ten-foot custom dining table, single slab. It took three of us just to get it off the flatbed truck and onto the sawhorses in the shop. The grain on this particular piece is just unbelievable, though. It has this crotch figure right near one end where the tree originally split into two branches, and it creates this sort of shimmering flame pattern in the wood. \n\nMost people these days just want to slather thick polyurethane all over a table like that, which completely kills the depth of the grain. I had to sit the clients down and explain that we have to do a hand-rubbed oil finish. You do a coat of pure tung oil, let it cure for a few days, buff it out with ultra-fine steel wool, and then do it all over again. It takes weeks to do it right, but the wood just ends up glowing from the inside out. Polyurethane basically just wraps the wood in cheap plastic. I will never understand why people pay thousands of dollars for a premium, historic piece of hardwood and then want it to feel like a Formica countertop from a diner. It completely defeats the purpose of having natural wood in your house. My old mentor, Frank, he used to refuse to even keep polyurethane in his shop. If a client asked for it, he'd show them the door. \n\nBut, oh, sorry. You were asking about feeling good about my body. Yeah, so... no, my weight isn't the only thing. I like that my arms are strong enough to handle those heavy slabs without dropping them. I guess I just appreciate being able to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control of your body or about how you think you'll look to others?\n\nPatient: Losing control, definitely. It's not really about how I look to anyone else. It's just... if I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight and just completely lose this grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of the holidays, my sister always hosts Thanksgiving now because I built her this massive dining table about ten years ago. It seats fourteen people easily. Solid white oak. White oak is incredible for dining furniture because it's a closed-grain wood, which makes it practically waterproof. That's actually why they use it to make whiskey barrels. You can spill a whole glass of red wine or gravy on that table and it just wipes right off without staining the fibers. \n\nI didn't use a single metal fastener on that entire piece, either. All traditional wooden joinery. I spent weeks cutting these huge mortise and tenon joints by hand with a Japanese pull saw and a set of antique chisels I bought at an estate sale. You really have to make sure your chisels are razor-sharp for that kind of work, or you'll just tear out the end grain and ruin the fit. I spend a good hour every Sunday just standing at my sharpening station with my water stones, working my way up to an 8000 grit stone until the steel edge is basically a mirror. It's tedious, sure, but when you slide a perfectly cut tenon into a mortise and it fits so tight you almost don't even need glue... there's just no feeling like it in the world. It's real, honest craftsmanship. \n\nBut um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, the weight thing. Yeah, it's just about keeping my grip on things, I guess. I don't want to lose that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I had to sit the clients down and explain that we have to do a hand-rubbed oil finish. You do a coat of pure tung oil, let it cure for a few days, buff it out with ultra-fine steel wool, and then do it all over again. It takes weeks to do it right, but the wood just ends up glowing from the inside out. Polyurethane basically just wraps the wood in cheap plastic. I will never understand why people pay thousands of dollars for a premium, historic piece of hardwood and then want it to feel like a Formica countertop from a diner. It completely defeats the purpose of having natural wood in your house. My old mentor, Frank, he used to refuse to even keep polyurethane in his shop. If a client asked for it, he'd show them the door. \n\nBut, oh, sorry. You were asking about feeling good about my body. Yeah, so... no, my weight isn't the only thing. I like that my arms are strong enough to handle those heavy slabs without dropping them. I guess I just appreciate being able to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control of your body or about how you think you'll look to others?\n\nPatient: Losing control, definitely. It's not really about how I look to anyone else. It's just... if I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight and just completely lose this grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of the holidays, my sister always hosts Thanksgiving now because I built her this massive dining table about ten years ago. It seats fourteen people easily. Solid white oak. White oak is incredible for dining furniture because it's a closed-grain wood, which makes it practically waterproof. That's actually why they use it to make whiskey barrels. You can spill a whole glass of red wine or gravy on that table and it just wipes right off without staining the fibers. \n\nI didn't use a single metal fastener on that entire piece, either. All traditional wooden joinery. I spent weeks cutting these huge mortise and tenon joints by hand with a Japanese pull saw and a set of antique chisels I bought at an estate sale. You really have to make sure your chisels are razor-sharp for that kind of work, or you'll just tear out the end grain and ruin the fit. I spend a good hour every Sunday just standing at my sharpening station with my water stones, working my way up to an 8000 grit stone until the steel edge is basically a mirror. It's tedious, sure, but when you slide a perfectly cut tenon into a mortise and it fits so tight you almost don't even need glue... there's just no feeling like it in the world. Look unacceptable? I mean, maybe a little, but it's really just like I was saying a minute ago. It's mostly about that control. If I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying immediately that I'm going to gain weight and lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. 

Speaking of old family things, my grandmother's house had these beautiful original double-hung sash windows, and I actually spent all of last month restoring a set just like them for a client in the historic district. People always want to rip out original wooden windows and put in those cheap vinyl replacements because some salesman convinced them it'll save on their heating bill. But vinyl only lasts maybe twenty years before the seals blow and they look terrible, and you can't repair them. A properly restored wooden window from 1920 will last another hundred years. 

You just have to know how to re-glaze them right. You have to carefully scrape out all the old, rock-hard putty—which usually takes a heat gun, a special scraper, and a whole lot of patience so you don't crack the antique wavy glass—and then you bed the pane in fresh linseed oil putty. And don't even get me started on the sash cords. I always replace the old broken cotton ropes with solid brass sash chain. It glides over the original cast-iron pulleys so smoothly. The counterweights are actually solid lead or cast iron, hidden inside the wall pockets. It's brilliant engineering, really. Just simple physics. You balance the weight of the sash perfectly with the counterweight in the wall, and you can lift a forty-pound wooden window with one finger. 

But wait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry about that. What did you ask me? Oh, right, looking unacceptable to myself. Yeah, it really just comes back to what I said before. It's about not losing my grip.

    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, my regular doctor sent me here. He says my weight has dropped too low and everyone is just making such a huge fuss about it lately. But honestly, I don't see what the big deal is. Being this light, it actually helps me keep up with everything at the shop. I don't get slowed down, which is important because I'm a carpenter and we are swamped right now. \n\nActually, we're doing this massive custom cabinetry job for a house out in the suburbs—beautiful cherry wood, really heavy stuff, but it has this gorgeous grain. I had to drive three hours just to source the right hardware for it last Tuesday because the client was being incredibly picky about the hinges. Antique brass, they said. Do you know how hard it is to find solid antique brass these days? Everything is just plated zinc now, it's terrible quality. You really have to know where to look. But anyway, yeah, I'm just here because my doctor wouldn't drop it about the weight thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean, yes, I think I'm perfectly fine. My husband and the apprentices at the shop keep saying I look too thin lately, but I keep telling them I’m fine and that people are just confusing discipline with sickness. \n\nSpeaking of the apprentices, though, they just have zero discipline these days. Take Tommy, for instance. Just last week he completely ruined a whole set of dovetail joints on that cherry wood job because he wouldn't take ten minutes to properly sharpen his chisels. I always tell him, a dull chisel is way more dangerous than a sharp one. My grandfather taught me that when I was twelve. He had this beautiful old Arkansas whetstone, passed down from his dad, and we’d spend hours on Sunday afternoons just oiling and sharpening all the hand tools until they could shave the hair off your arm. You just don't see that kind of care anymore, you know? Everyone just grabs a power router and blasts through the wood. It makes such a mess of the workshop, dust everywhere... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, the weight thing. But yeah, like I said, I'm completely fine.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... if I eat anything heavy, or if I let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight. I just feel like I'd lose the grip I’ve worked so incredibly hard to keep, you know? \n\nBut speaking of the holidays, we used to have these huge family dinners on this massive red oak table my uncle built. That table is actually what got me into carpentry in the first place. It had these beautifully turned legs, just solid, heavy wood. You really don't see red oak used like that much anymore because it's an absolute nightmare to transport, and the freight costs just completely eat into your profit margins. We had a couple in the shop just last month asking for a ten-foot solid oak farmhouse table, and I had to sit them down and explain that the shipping alone for the lumber would cost more than their chairs. People just don't understand logistics these days. They look at a picture on the internet and think they can just get it delivered next-day like it's a pair of shoes. \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Gaining weight. But yeah, like I said, I just can't risk eating those heavy foods and losing my grip on things.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: Unattractive? I don't... I don't know if that's the right word. I just know I wouldn't look like *me* anymore. I've been this way for so long, and it's just how I'm built now. \n\nIt's kind of like... well, it's like when you try to restore a piece of furniture and someone has slapped a thick coat of cheap latex paint over a beautiful mahogany veneer. We get those in the shop all the time. People find these incredible mid-century dressers at estate sales, and some DIYer thought it would look \"cute\" painted seafoam green. It just completely hides the true lines of the piece, you know? It takes hours and hours of stripping and sanding just to get down to the bare wood so you can actually see the craftsmanship again. The chemicals we have to use for that stripping process are just awful, too. You have to wear these heavy-duty respirators, and even then, the fumes give you this splitting headache by the end of the day. Tommy, my apprentice, he refuses to wear the respirator properly. He just lets it hang around his neck. I keep telling him he's going to ruin his lungs before he's thirty, but kids today just think they're invincible. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about looking unattractive. Yeah, I just... I don't really think about it like that. I just want to look like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that gaining weight would change who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: Change who I am? I... I suppose it would. I mean, my discipline, my routine, it's all tied up in how I maintain myself. If I lost that control, I just wouldn't be the same person. \n\nIt's like when you lose the true square on a project. Once your foundation is off, everything else you build on top of it is just going to be crooked. I was actually just explaining this to my husband the other day when we were trying to fix the deck out back. The original contractors didn't set the footings below the frost line, so over the last ten winters, the whole structure has just heaved and warped. You can't just slap new decking boards on top of that and call it a day. You have to dig down, pour new concrete, and start from scratch. It's exhausting work, mixing concrete by hand in a wheelbarrow. We used to have this great little electric mixer, but the motor burnt out last spring when Tommy tried to mix a batch that was way too dry. I swear, that boy costs me more in broken equipment than he makes me in labor. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm rambling again. What did you ask me? Something about changing who I am? But yes, I think it would change me. I just need things to stay exactly how they are.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly about not being able to control your eating or your body?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think it's both, really. Like I said before... if I let myself have those heavy holiday foods, or anything rich like that, I just start panicking that I'm going to gain weight and completely lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. It's all about keeping that grip. \n\nIt reminds me of these bar clamps we use in the shop. You know, the really heavy-duty steel ones? When you're gluing up a big tabletop, you need absolute, unyielding pressure. If the clamp slips even a fraction of an inch, the whole joint is compromised. We had this huge batch of clamps we bought at an auction a few years ago, from this old cabinet maker who was retiring. Beautiful old tools, made back when they actually used quality steel. But half of them had these stripped threads. I spent three whole weekends trying to rethread them because I just couldn't stand the idea of throwing away good American steel. My husband kept telling me to just buy new ones, but the new ones they sell at the big box stores are just cheap aluminum garbage. You tighten them down and they just bow in the middle. You can't trust them to hold anything together when it really matters. \n\nWait, what were you asking me? Oh, about controlling my eating. Yes, exactly. If I let go, even a little, I just worry I'll lose the grip on everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your weight is the one thing in your life that you can completely control?\n\nPatient: Control... yeah, I suppose it is. Everything else is just so unpredictable, you know? But my weight, what I eat, that's mine. I can measure it, I can manage it. \n\nIt's a lot like working with a good piece of hard maple. Maple is stubborn, but if your tools are sharp and you know what you're doing, it'll do exactly what you want it to. You can measure it down to the thirty-second of an inch and it holds its shape. Unlike pine. I hate working with pine. It's so soft, it dents if you just look at it wrong, and it warps the second the humidity changes. We had this client last month who insisted on a custom dining table made out of knotty pine because she saw it on some home renovation show. I tried to talk her out of it, told her it wouldn't hold up to daily use, especially with her three golden retrievers running around the house. Those dogs were absolute menaces, by the way. When we went over to take measurements, one of them chewed right through the cord of my orbital sander. I had to splice the wire back together in my truck just so we could finish the job. And the client didn't even apologize! Just laughed and said he was \"teething.\" He was a full-grown dog! \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, about control. Yeah, I just don't really know if it's the *only* thing I can control, but it's definitely the most important one.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear gaining weight because you think it would make you feel fat?\n\nPatient: Feel fat? I... well, yes. It's like I was saying before about the holiday foods. If I eat anything heavy, anything rich like we used to make, I start worrying right away that I'm going to gain weight. And then I'll just feel... big. Soft. Like I've lost that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep.\n\nIt's a lot like when wood gets waterlogged. You know, like if you leave a piece of untreated timber out in the rain? It just swells up and gets spongy. You can't work with it anymore. It won't take a clean edge, the saw blade just tears at it instead of cutting. We had this whole pallet of oak delivered last spring, and the delivery driver—this new kid who couldn't have been more than nineteen—just left it sitting right at the end of the driveway instead of bringing it under the awning. Of course, we got a freak thunderstorm that afternoon. Three inches of rain in two hours. The whole bottom layer of that pallet was ruined. Just soaked through. Sourced all the insulation panels from a demolition site downtown. They were tearing down this beautiful old theater to build another generic apartment complex. It breaks my heart to see that kind of craftsmanship just bulldozed. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Getting off track. But yes, I worry about gaining weight. You go onto any job site, and you see these young kids tossing their chisels into a bucket like they're scrap metal. A good chisel, a really good high-carbon steel chisel, needs to be respected. You don't get hands like these unless you put in the hours and actually work for it. \n\nLike last week, I was doing this restoration on a beautiful Victorian vanity. Solid walnut. The client kept calling it \"mid-century vintage\" and I had to bite my tongue because, honestly, people just throw these words around without knowing the history of furniture making. They use these engineered I-joists now instead of solid lumber, and they wonder why the floors bounce when you walk across the room. Back in the day, you'd use true dimensional lumber, rough-sawn fir or pine, and those houses are still standing a hundred years later without a single sag in the roofline. Now it's all oriented strand board and cheap glue. It's just disrespectful to the trades.\n\nWait, I'm doing it again. What was the question? Control? Right. Yeah, losing my grip on my weight. The guy who passed away, his widow was just selling off his entire shop for pennies. It broke my heart. She had a whole box of vintage auger bits marked for five dollars. Five dollars! Those things are worth their weight in gold if you know how to file the spurs right. I bought the whole box, of course, and the plane. Baltic birch is the best for cabinet boxes, hands down. It has more plies than standard plywood, no voids in the inner layers, so when you cut it, the edge looks clean and it holds screws perfectly. It takes weeks to do it right, but the wood just ends up glowing from the inside out. Polyurethane basically just wraps the wood in cheap plastic. I will never understand why people pay thousands of dollars for a premium, historic piece of hardwood and then want it to feel like a Formica countertop from a diner. I spend a good hour every Sunday just standing at my sharpening station with my water stones, working my way up to an 8000 grit stone until the steel edge is basically a mirror. It's tedious, sure, but when you slide a perfectly cut tenon into a mortise and it fits so tight you almost don't even need glue... there's just no feeling like it in the world. People always want to rip out original wooden windows and put in those cheap vinyl replacements because some salesman convinced them it'll save on their heating bill. But vinyl only lasts maybe twenty years before the seals blow and they look terrible, and you can't repair them. A properly restored wooden window from 1920 will last another hundred years. \n\nYou just have to know how to re-glaze them right. The counterweights are actually solid lead or cast iron, hidden inside the wall pockets. It's brilliant engineering, really. Just simple physics. You know, like if you leave a piece of untreated timber out in the rain? It just swells up and gets spongy. You can't work with it anymore. It won't take a clean edge, the saw blade just tears at it instead of cutting. We had this whole pallet of oak delivered last spring, and the delivery driver—this new kid who couldn't have been more than nineteen—just left it sitting right at the end of the driveway instead of bringing it under the awning. Of course, we got a freak thunderstorm that afternoon. Three inches of rain in two hours. The whole bottom layer of that pallet was ruined. Just soaked through. I tried putting it in the kiln we built in the corner of the shop, but it was too far gone. It took me a week just to build that kiln, by the way. Sourced all the insulation panels from a demolition site downtown. They were tearing down this beautiful old theater to build another generic apartment complex. It breaks my heart to see that kind of craftsmanship just bulldozed. \n\nI'm sorry, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Getting off track. But yes, I worry about gaining weight. I just can't stand the thought of feeling heavy or losing my grip on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat now?\n\nPatient: Look fat now? No, no I don't think I do. I mean, my husband and the apprentices at the shop keep telling me I look too thin, but I keep telling them I'm perfectly fine. People just confuse discipline with sickness these days. Nobody understands what it takes to actually maintain standards anymore. \n\nIt's just like how people treat their tools now. You go onto any job site, and you see these young kids tossing their chisels into a bucket like they're scrap metal. A good chisel, a really good high-carbon steel chisel, needs to be respected. You have to keep it oiled, you have to hone the edge after every major job. I have a set of Marples chisels I bought back in 1988, the ones with the split-proof handles. They don't even make them in Sheffield anymore, they moved production overseas and the quality just plummeted. But my set? I can still shave the hair off my arm with them. I spend at least an hour every Sunday evening just at my sharpening station. I built it myself, actually, out of some reclaimed mahogany I salvaged from an old bank lobby they were gutting. Beautiful wood, tight grain. The bank manager just wanted it all thrown in the dumpster so they could put up drywall and glass. Can you believe that? Throwing away century-old mahogany! \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my weight. Yeah, they say I'm too thin, but they just don't get it. I'm fine. It's just discipline.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only part of your body that you're satisfied with?\n\nPatient: The only part? I... well, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I'm actually pretty satisfied with my hands. They're strong. Look at these calluses. You don't get hands like these unless you put in the hours and actually work for it. \n\nLike last week, I was doing this restoration on a beautiful Victorian vanity. Solid walnut. The client kept calling it \"mid-century vintage\" and I had to bite my tongue because, honestly, people just throw these words around without knowing the history of furniture making. Anyway, the dovetail joints on the drawers were starting to separate, and I had to carefully knock them apart with a rubber mallet. You have to be so incredibly gentle, or you'll crack the pins, and then you've ruined a piece of history. And the brass pulls on it! They were completely black with tarnish. I took them down to this little hardware store on 4th Street to see if I could find matching screws because a few of the original ones were stripped. \n\nHave you ever been to that store? Miller's Hardware? Well, don't bother looking for it now, because they just sold the building to a developer. It's going to be a boutique pet bakery or something ridiculous like that. Fifty years of a family business, just gone, all so people can buy organic cupcakes for their poodles. I went in there to say goodbye to the owner, Frank, and he just looked so tired. It's just... it's a real shame what's happening to the neighborhood. \n\nBut um... I'm sorry, what did you ask me? Something about my body? Oh, right, being satisfied with it. Yeah, I don't know about my weight being the *only* thing. I just don't really think about the rest of it that much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think gaining weight would make you feel like you're losing control over your body?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, exactly. It's like I mentioned earlier... if I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have those rich foods we used to make for the holidays, I just start panicking right away that I'm going to gain weight. It absolutely feels like I'm losing that grip I've worked so hard to keep. \n\nThose holiday foods, though... my mother used to make this incredibly dense plum pudding. She'd steam it in this huge copper basin. Actually, copper is such a nightmare to maintain. We had a client last month who wanted a custom kitchen island with a hammered copper countertop. I tried to talk him out of it. I said, look, the patina is going to change unevenly unless you are meticulous about wiping up acid spills, like lemon juice or tomato sauce. But he wouldn't listen. He's an investment banker, one of those guys who wears a fleece vest over a dress shirt and thinks he knows everything about everything. \n\nHe had this massive house up on the ridge, the kind with the fake stone veneer on the front that looks like it's going to peel off in a strong wind. I swear, the structural integrity of these new builds is an absolute joke. They use these engineered I-joists now instead of solid lumber, and they wonder why the floors bounce when you walk across the room. Back in the day, you'd use true dimensional lumber, rough-sawn fir or pine, and those houses are still standing a hundred years later without a single sag in the roofline. Now it's all oriented strand board and cheap glue. It's just disrespectful to the trades.\n\nWait, I'm doing it again. What was the question? Control? Right. Yeah, losing my grip on my weight. That's exactly it. I just can't let that happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that you are happy with about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or something really similar? About being satisfied with my body? I think I just said I really like my hands because they're strong from all the work I do. \n\nSpeaking of strong hands, you know what really takes hand strength? Hand-planing a piece of rough-cut oak down to a smooth finish. Most guys these days just run it through a thickness planer, flip a switch, and let the machine do the work. It's so loud, you have to wear those heavy earmuffs, and it just throws sawdust everywhere. But using a hand plane... a really good Stanley No. 4, maybe one from the 1930s before they started cheaping out on the castings... that's an art. You can feel the grain of the wood through the handles. You push it forward, and you get this beautiful, continuous ribbon of wood shaving coming up through the mouth of the plane. It sounds like a zipper, just this satisfying *shhhhck* sound. \n\nI found a gorgeous old Stanley at an estate sale last month. The guy who passed away, his widow was just selling off his entire shop for pennies. It broke my heart. She had a whole box of vintage auger bits marked for five dollars. Five dollars! Those things are worth their weight in gold if you know how to file the spurs right. I bought the whole box, of course, and the plane. The iron on the plane was completely rusted, but I soaked it in Evapo-Rust overnight and then spent three hours on my water stones bringing the edge back. \n\nSorry, I'm rambling about tools again. But yeah, you asked about my appearance and what I'm happy with. Like I said before, I don't really think my weight is the only thing. My hands are good. I just don't really focus on the rest of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the main thing that determines how attractive you are to others?\n\nPatient: Attractive to others? I... I don't really know. I don't think about being attractive to other people very much. I mean, my weight is important to me, but I don't know if that's what other people care about. \n\nHonestly, most of the people I interact with are clients, and they only care if I can finish their custom cabinets on time. Which, by the way, is getting harder and harder because getting good materials right now is a nightmare. I ordered some Baltic birch plywood three weeks ago from my usual supplier. Baltic birch is the best for cabinet boxes, hands down. It has more plies than standard plywood, no voids in the inner layers, so when you cut it, the edge looks clean and it holds screws perfectly. But my supplier called me on Tuesday and said it's backordered until next month, and offered me this cheap MDF core stuff instead. \n\nI told him, absolutely not. MDF is basically just heavy cardboard glued together. If it gets even a drop of water on it, it swells up like a sponge and you can never fix it. And it's so heavy! Trying to lift a 4x8 sheet of 3/4-inch MDF by yourself is a great way to throw out your back. I knew a guy, Tommy, used to work framing houses. He tried to muscle a sheet of MDF up a flight of stairs by himself, slipped, and the whole thing came down on him. Tore his rotator cuff. He had to be out of work for six months, and his wife had to take an extra shift at the diner just to pay their mortgage. \n\nAnyway, I had to drive two hours out of town to a specialty lumber yard just to find decent plywood. Cost me a fortune in gas, too. \n\nBut um... attractiveness? Yeah, I really couldn't tell you what makes me attractive to others. I just don't pay attention to that kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the main thing that determines your overall health?\n\nPatient: My overall health? I mean... I don't really know. I guess it's a part of it, but I don't really think of it as the main thing. I try to stay active, keep moving, you know? \n\nSpeaking of moving, my knees have been killing me lately. I think it's from kneeling on concrete floors all week. We're doing this big restoration job on an old Victorian over on Elm Street. The floors were completely shot—water damage, termite rot, you name it. We had to rip up all the original heart pine, which was a crying shame. Heart pine is practically extinct now. You can only get it reclaimed from old factories and barns. The grain is so tight, and it has this deep, rich amber color that you just can't fake with modern stains. \n\nAnyway, the homeowner wanted us to lay down this new pre-finished engineered hardwood. I tried to explain to her that putting engineered wood in a house from 1890 is practically a crime against architecture. It just looks too perfect, too uniform. A house that old needs character, it needs imperfections. But she was dead set on it because she saw it on some home renovation show. Those shows are the worst thing to ever happen to my profession. They make it look like you can gut a kitchen and rebuild it in three days for five thousand dollars. They never show the plumbing nightmares, or the knob-and-tube wiring you have to rip out to bring things up to code. \n\nI spent three days last week just trying to level the subfloor in that house. The joists had sagged almost two inches in the center of the room. You can't just slap new flooring over that, or it'll feel like you're walking on a trampoline. I had to sister every single joist, which meant crawling around in a crawlspace that was maybe two feet high, covered in century-old dust and spider webs. \n\nBut um, what were we talking about? Health? Yeah, I don't know if my weight is the main thing. I just try to keep working. As long as I can still swing a hammer, I figure I'm doing okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the only thing that makes you feel good about your body?\n\nPatient: The only thing? No, I don't think so. I mean, I know I brought up my hands earlier, but I guess I feel pretty good about my arms and shoulders, too. You kind of have to have solid upper body strength to do what I do. \n\nLike just yesterday, I was maneuvering these massive slabs of live-edge black walnut. Have you ever seen raw black walnut? It's absolutely gorgeous, but it is incredibly dense and heavy. This client wants a ten-foot custom dining table, single slab. It took three of us just to get it off the flatbed truck and onto the sawhorses in the shop. The grain on this particular piece is just unbelievable, though. It has this crotch figure right near one end where the tree originally split into two branches, and it creates this sort of shimmering flame pattern in the wood. \n\nMost people these days just want to slather thick polyurethane all over a table like that, which completely kills the depth of the grain. I had to sit the clients down and explain that we have to do a hand-rubbed oil finish. You do a coat of pure tung oil, let it cure for a few days, buff it out with ultra-fine steel wool, and then do it all over again. It takes weeks to do it right, but the wood just ends up glowing from the inside out. Polyurethane basically just wraps the wood in cheap plastic. I will never understand why people pay thousands of dollars for a premium, historic piece of hardwood and then want it to feel like a Formica countertop from a diner. It completely defeats the purpose of having natural wood in your house. My old mentor, Frank, he used to refuse to even keep polyurethane in his shop. If a client asked for it, he'd show them the door. \n\nBut, oh, sorry. You were asking about feeling good about my body. Yeah, so... no, my weight isn't the only thing. I like that my arms are strong enough to handle those heavy slabs without dropping them. I guess I just appreciate being able to do my job.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about feeling like you're losing control of your body or about how you think you'll look to others?\n\nPatient: Losing control, definitely. It's not really about how I look to anyone else. It's just... if I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have the kinds of foods we used to make for the holidays, I start worrying right away that I’ll gain weight and just completely lose this grip I’ve worked so hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of the holidays, my sister always hosts Thanksgiving now because I built her this massive dining table about ten years ago. It seats fourteen people easily. Solid white oak. White oak is incredible for dining furniture because it's a closed-grain wood, which makes it practically waterproof. That's actually why they use it to make whiskey barrels. You can spill a whole glass of red wine or gravy on that table and it just wipes right off without staining the fibers. \n\nI didn't use a single metal fastener on that entire piece, either. All traditional wooden joinery. I spent weeks cutting these huge mortise and tenon joints by hand with a Japanese pull saw and a set of antique chisels I bought at an estate sale. You really have to make sure your chisels are razor-sharp for that kind of work, or you'll just tear out the end grain and ruin the fit. I spend a good hour every Sunday just standing at my sharpening station with my water stones, working my way up to an 8000 grit stone until the steel edge is basically a mirror. It's tedious, sure, but when you slide a perfectly cut tenon into a mortise and it fits so tight you almost don't even need glue... there's just no feeling like it in the world. It's real, honest craftsmanship. \n\nBut um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, the weight thing. Yeah, it's just about keeping my grip on things, I guess. I don't want to lose that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unacceptable to yourself if you were heavier?\n\nPatient: Look unacceptable? I mean, maybe a little, but it's really just like I was saying a minute ago. It's mostly about that control. If I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying immediately that I'm going to gain weight and lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of old family things, my grandmother's house had these beautiful original double-hung sash windows, and I actually spent all of last month restoring a set just like them for a client in the historic district. People always want to rip out original wooden windows and put in those cheap vinyl replacements because some salesman convinced them it'll save on their heating bill. But vinyl only lasts maybe twenty years before the seals blow and they look terrible, and you can't repair them. A properly restored wooden window from 1920 will last another hundred years. \n\nYou just have to know how to re-glaze them right. You have to carefully scrape out all the old, rock-hard putty—which usually takes a heat gun, a special scraper, and a whole lot of patience so you don't crack the antique wavy glass—and then you bed the pane in fresh linseed oil putty. And don't even get me started on the sash cords. I always replace the old broken cotton ropes with solid brass sash chain. It glides over the original cast-iron pulleys so smoothly. The counterweights are actually solid lead or cast iron, hidden inside the wall pockets. It's brilliant engineering, really. Just simple physics. You balance the weight of the sash perfectly with the counterweight in the wall, and you can lift a forty-pound wooden window with one finger. \n\nBut wait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry about that. What did you ask me? Oh, right, looking unacceptable to myself. Yeah, it really just comes back to what I said before. It's about not losing my grip.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I spend a good hour every Sunday just standing at my sharpening station with my water stones, working my way up to an 8000 grit stone until the steel edge is basically a mirror. It's tedious, sure, but when you slide a perfectly cut tenon into a mortise and it fits so tight you almost don't even need glue... there's just no feeling like it in the world. It's real, honest craftsmanship. \n\nBut um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, the weight thing. Yeah, it's just about keeping my grip on things, I guess. People always want to rip out original wooden windows and put in those cheap vinyl replacements because some salesman convinced them it'll save on their heating bill. But vinyl only lasts maybe twenty years before the seals blow and they look terrible, and you can't repair them. A properly restored wooden window from 1920 will last another hundred years. \n\nYou just have to know how to re-glaze them right. You have to carefully scrape out all the old, rock-hard putty—which usually takes a heat gun, a special scraper, and a whole lot of patience so you don't crack the antique wavy glass—and then you bed the pane in fresh linseed oil putty. And don't even get me started on the sash cords. I always replace the old broken cotton ropes with solid brass sash chain. It glides over the original cast-iron pulleys so smoothly. The counterweights are actually solid lead or cast iron, hidden inside the wall pockets. It's brilliant engineering, really. Just simple physics. You balance the weight of the sash perfectly with the counterweight in the wall, and you can lift a forty-pound wooden window with one finger. \n\nBut wait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
The main reason? I don't know if I'd say it's the *main* reason. I mean, I don't really think about it like that. I guess I just don't really know if it's the main reason or not. 

But, you know, speaking of looks, I was actually just looking at the blueprints for a custom library build I'm starting next week. The client wants floor-to-ceiling built-in bookcases across three walls, complete with a rolling ladder. It's a dream job, honestly. We're doing the whole thing in cherry wood. Cherry is fascinating because when you first mill it, it's this very pale, almost pinkish color. It doesn't look like much at all. But as soon as it's exposed to UV light, it starts to oxidize and deepen into this incredibly rich, warm reddish-brown. 

A lot of commercial furniture makers will stain cherry to make it look dark right away, but that's a huge mistake. If you stain it, you end up muddying the grain, and then when the wood naturally darkens underneath the stain over time, it looks terrible. You just have to be patient. I tell my clients to keep everything out of direct sunlight for the first six months, or you'll get these stark tan lines where a book or a picture frame was sitting. You have to let the wood age naturally. I'm actually designing a custom track system for the rolling ladder out of solid brass, which I'm going to antique myself using a chemical patina solution so it matches the aged look the cherry will eventually get. It's going to be stunning. 

But um... sorry, I lost track again. You were asking about my weight being the main reason I like how I look? Yeah, I just... I'm not really sure if it's the main reason. I haven't really thought about it that way.

I spend a good hour every Sunday just standing at my sharpening station with my water stones, working my way up to an 8000 grit stone until the steel edge is basically a mirror. It's tedious, sure, but when you slide a perfectly cut tenon into a mortise and it fits so tight you almost don't even need glue... there's just no feeling like it in the world. It's real, honest craftsmanship. \n\nBut um... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, the weight thing. Yeah, it's just about keeping my grip on things, I guess. I don't want to lose that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unacceptable to yourself if you were heavier?\n\nPatient: Look unacceptable? I mean, maybe a little, but it's really just like I was saying a minute ago. It's mostly about that control. If I eat anything heavy, or, you know, let myself have those rich holiday foods we used to make, I just start worrying immediately that I'm going to gain weight and lose that grip I've worked so incredibly hard to keep. \n\nSpeaking of old family things, my grandmother's house had these beautiful original double-hung sash windows, and I actually spent all of last month restoring a set just like them for a client in the historic district. People always want to rip out original wooden windows and put in those cheap vinyl replacements because some salesman convinced them it'll save on their heating bill. But vinyl only lasts maybe twenty years before the seals blow and they look terrible, and you can't repair them. A properly restored wooden window from 1920 will last another hundred years. \n\nYou just have to know how to re-glaze them right. You have to carefully scrape out all the old, rock-hard putty—which usually takes a heat gun, a special scraper, and a whole lot of patience so you don't crack the antique wavy glass—and then you bed the pane in fresh linseed oil putty. And don't even get me started on the sash cords. I always replace the old broken cotton ropes with solid brass sash chain. It glides over the original cast-iron pulleys so smoothly. The counterweights are actually solid lead or cast iron, hidden inside the wall pockets. It's brilliant engineering, really. Just simple physics. You balance the weight of the sash perfectly with the counterweight in the wall, and you can lift a forty-pound wooden window with one finger. \n\nBut wait, I'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry about that. What did you ask me? Oh, right, looking unacceptable to myself. Yeah, it really just comes back to what I said before. It's about not losing my grip.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the main reason you like the way you look?\n\nPatient: The main reason? I don't know if I'd say it's the *main* reason. The client wants floor-to-ceiling built-in bookcases across three walls, complete with a rolling ladder. It's a dream job, honestly. We're doing the whole thing in cherry wood. Cherry is fascinating because when you first mill it, it's this very pale, almost pinkish color. It doesn't look like much at all. But as soon as it's exposed to UV light, it starts to oxidize and deepen into this incredibly rich, warm reddish-brown. \n\nA lot of commercial furniture makers will stain cherry to make it look dark right away, but that's a huge mistake. If you stain it, you end up muddying the grain, and then when the wood naturally darkens underneath the stain over time, it looks terrible. You just have to be patient. I tell my clients to keep everything out of direct sunlight for the first six months, or you'll get these stark tan lines where a book or a picture frame was sitting. You have to let the wood age naturally. I'm actually designing a custom track system for the rolling ladder out of solid brass, which I'm going to antique myself using a chemical patina solution so it matches the aged look the cherry will eventually get. It's going to be stunning. \n\nBut um... sorry, I lost track again. You were asking about my weight being the main reason I like how I look? Yeah, I just... A distorted view? I... I don't think so. I mean, I see what's there. I look in the mirror and I just see my body, you know? I don't think my view is distorted or anything like that. 

Though, talking about views and looking at things properly... I was just at the lumber yard this morning picking through a lift of walnut for a custom credenza, and you really have to know how to look at the boards. Most people just look at the face grain, but you have to look at the end grain to see how the board was milled. If you want something really stable, especially for a wide top, you need quarter-sawn lumber. The growth rings run perfectly perpendicular to the face of the board, which means it barely moves when the humidity changes. 

Plain-sawn boards—where the rings look like arches on the end—they cup like crazy. I had a guy bring me a table a few years ago that he bought from some big-box store, and the top had cupped so badly it looked like a skateboard ramp. They had just edge-glued a bunch of cheap, plain-sawn boards together and then locked them down to a metal base without allowing for seasonal expansion. Wood is basically a bundle of microscopic straws, right? Even after it's kiln-dried, it still breathes. It takes on moisture in the summer and expands, and dries out in the winter and shrinks. If you don't account for that movement—like using elongated screw holes or figure-eight fasteners—the wood will literally tear itself apart. It's incredibly powerful. You can't force wood to stay still; you have to work with it, let it move the way it wants to... 

Wait, what was the question? Something about a distorted view? Yeah, I don't... I don't think I have a distorted view. I just see what's actually there.
